============================================================================
11.3 SSH CHAOS MONKEY (INTERMITTENT MICRO-NETWORK CUTS)
============================================================================

Methodology[cite: 3]:

- Remote backup via SSH with twenty 32 KB files each[cite: 3].
- The child SSH process is located via pgrep and subjected to
  5 cycles of SIGSTOP (0.5s frozen) / SIGCONT (0.5s resumed)[cite: 3].

- This simulates intermittent cable cuts that force the C code
  to handle EAGAIN/EWOULDBLOCK mid-stream without deadlock[cite: 3].

- A 120-second timeout protects against infinite hangs[cite: 3].
- After chaos, "baresnap verify" validates repository integrity[cite: 3].
Result: 5/5 tests passed[cite: 3].

- Create survived all micro-cuts (rc=0)[cite: 3].
- Repository fully intact after verify[cite: 3].
- The binary protocol over SSH handles frozen/resumed connections
  without corruption, deadlock, or data loss[cite: 3].

============================================================================
11.4 ENOSPC: DISK SPACE FAILURE
============================================================================

Methodology[cite: 3]:

- Use `ulimit -f` to limit maximum file size to 1 MB[cite: 3].
- Attempt to create a 2 MB snapshot (impossible due to space)[cite: 3].
- Subsequent verification with "baresnap verify"[cite: 3].
Result: 5/5 tests passed[cite: 3].

- BareSnap detects the space limit during pack writing[cite: 3].
- Aborts cleanly with: "error: cannot finalize pack — aborting create"[cite: 3]
- The repository remains intact and verifiable after failure, without requiring
  root (sudo) privileges[cite: 3].

============================================================================
11.5 EXTREME CONCURRENCY (APPEND-ONLY)
============================================================================

Methodology[cite: 3]:

- 5 baresnap create processes writing simultaneously to the same
  repository, each with its own data source[cite: 3].

- Subsequent verification with "baresnap verify"[cite: 3].
Result: 6/6 tests passed[cite: 3].

- All 5 processes completed successfully[cite: 3].
- 5 independent snapshots were created[cite: 3].
- The repository remained intact after verify[cite: 3].

Demonstrates that the append-only architecture supports concurrent writes
without requiring exclusive locking, similar to Restic and Borg[cite: 3].

============================================================================
11.6 SATELLITE CONNECTION (HOSTILE NETWORK)
============================================================================

Methodology[cite: 3]:

- SSH wrapper throttling bandwidth to 50 KB/s using `pv`[cite: 3].
- Active SSH keepalives (ServerAliveInterval=15) to prevent
  premature disconnections[cite: 3].

- Backup of 600 KB (30 files) over this simulated connection[cite: 3].
Result: 5/5 tests passed[cite: 3].

- Backup completed in ~15 seconds (vs ~2 seconds on local network)[cite: 3].
- Remote repository intact after verify[cite: 3].
Demonstrates that the binary protocol over SSH withstands extreme network
conditions without corruption or hangs[cite: 3].

============================================================================
11.7 POWER CUT: KILL -9 DURING PRUNE
============================================================================

Methodology[cite: 3]:

- Repository with 5 snapshots and multiple packs[cite: 3].
- "baresnap prune --keep-last 1" launched in background[cite: 3].
- Process killed with SIGKILL at 10 ms (mid-operation)[cite: 3].
- Immediately after: "baresnap health" + auto-repair[cite: 3].
- Verification: zero orphaned .tmp, verify passes, repo usable[cite: 3].
Result: 8/8 tests passed[cite: 3].

- Health executed cleanly after kill -9 (rc=0)[cite: 3].
- Zero zombie temporaries in tmp/[cite: 3].
- Verify OK after kill -9 + repair[cite: 3].
- Post-kill create works normally[cite: 3].
- Complete post-kill audit clean (packs, idx, blm consistent)[cite: 3].
Demonstrates that the write-before-delete pattern and atomic rename
guarantee repository survival even under forced process
termination[cite: 3].

============================================================================
11.8 BIT ROT INJECTION (CRC32C DETECTION)
============================================================================

Methodology[cite: 3]:

- A single byte (0xDE) injected into the middle of a pack file via dd[cite: 3].
- "baresnap verify" must detect the CRC32C mismatch[cite: 3].
- "baresnap health" must detect the corrupted pack (rc=1)[cite: 3].
Result: 6/6 tests passed[cite: 3].

- Verify detects bit rot with integrity message[cite: 3].
- Health detects corrupted pack (rc=1)[cite: 3].
- No silent data corruption possible[cite: 3].

============================================================================
11.9 PACK UNIQUENESS GUARANTEE (64-BIT ID)
============================================================================

To avoid race conditions, collisions in massive virtualized
environments, or mismatches due to NTP clock synchronization drift,
BareSnap implements an identifier scheme based on 64-bit IDs
generated with kernel entropy, replacing nanosecond timestamps[cite: 3].

Mechanism: The engine extracts 8 bytes of pure entropy directly from the
Linux kernel using the getrandom() system call (or /dev/urandom
as a secure fallback)[cite: 3]. The result is interpreted as a little-endian uint64_t[cite: 3].

Failure probability: The mathematical collision probability is reduced to
1 in 2^64 (≈ 1.8 × 10¹⁹), shielding repository integrity
against any burst of concurrency[cite: 3].

C11 reference implementation[cite: 3]:

    #include <stdint.h>
    #include <stddef.h>
    #include <sys/random.h>
    uint64_t brs_generate_pack_id(void) {
        uint64_t id;
        ssize_t ret;
        do { ret = getrandom(&id, sizeof(id), 0); } while (ret < (ssize_t)sizeof(id));
        return id;
    }[cite: 3]

Test methodology[cite: 3]:

- 10 snapshots created in rapid succession (no sleep between them)[cite: 3].
- All pack IDs verified as unique (no collisions)[cite: 3].
- Prune --keep-last 2 and full consistency audit[cite: 3].
Result: 7/7 tests passed[cite: 3].

- 10 unique pack IDs generated under burst conditions[cite: 3].
- Prune and verify pass without issues[cite: 3].
- Clean post-prune audit (packs, idx, blm, tmp consistent)[cite: 3].

============================================================================
11.10 POST-PRUNE AUDIT (IDX/PACK CONSISTENCY)
============================================================================

Methodology[cite: 3]:

- 5 snapshots created, then prune --keep-last 1[cite: 3].
- Cross-verification: the number of .idx files on disk must
  match the segments reported by "baresnap info"[cite: 3].

- Each .blm must have its corresponding .idx and vice versa[cite: 3].
- Zero orphaned .tmp files allowed[cite: 3].
Result: 7/7 tests passed[cite: 3].

- Clean audit: 1 pack, 1 idx, 1 blm, 0 tmp[cite: 3].
- Info reports the correct number of segments[cite: 3].
- Verify passes after prune[cite: 3].
Demonstrates that prune never leaves the repository in an
inconsistent state, even under aggressive retention policies[cite: 3].

============================================================================
11.11 PHANTOM PERMISSION: CHMOD 000 MID-CREATE
============================================================================

Methodology[cite: 3]:

- 500 small files created as source[cite: 3].
- "baresnap create" launched in background[cite: 3].
- After 20 ms, chmod 000 applied to half the files (250)[cite: 3].
- Create must handle EACCES cleanly: skip unreadable files,
  log warnings, complete snapshot with remaining files[cite: 3].

- No crash, no corrupted pack, no orphaned temporaries[cite: 3].
Result: 5/5 tests passed[cite: 3].

- Create completed with 250 chmod 000 files[cite: 3].
- Repository intact after verify[cite: 3].
- Zero orphaned temporaries in tmp/[cite: 3].
- Snapshot created correctly (partial but consistent)[cite: 3].
Demonstrates that the packer handles EACCES in the hot path without
crashing or leaving the pack in a corrupted state[cite: 3].

============================================================================
11.12 MEMORY ANALYSIS (VALGRIND / ASAN / TSAN)
============================================================================
Tools: Valgrind 3.18.1 (Memcheck), AddressSanitizer (ASan),
UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer (UBSan), and ThreadSanitizer (TSan)[cite: 3].

Methodology[cite: 3]:
- Binary compiled with gcc -g -O0 for Valgrind[cite: 3].
- Binary compiled with -fsanitize=address,undefined for ASan/UBSan[cite: 3].
- Binary compiled with -fsanitize=thread for TSan (compile_tsan.sh)[cite: 3].
- Tests on empty repos and with 10 MB of random data (/dev/urandom)[cite: 3].

Valgrind modules integrated into mega_test (v2.3.5)[cite: 3]:
Modules 104, 105, and 106 execute each data operation (init, create,
verify, restore, prune) under valgrind --leak-check=full --show-leak-kinds=all
and audit the resulting log with check_vg_log()[cite: 3]. Pass criteria:
"definitely lost: 0 bytes" and "ERROR SUMMARY: 0 errors from 0 contexts"[cite: 3].

Result[cite: 3]:
- Valgrind (modules 104/105/106): 0 leaks, 0 errors in ALL phases[cite: 3].
- ASan/UBSan: 58/58 tests passing, zero memory errors, zero
  use-after-free, zero buffer overflows, zero undefined behavior[cite: 3].
- TSan: 0 data races[cite: 3].
- 27 allocs, 27 frees in empty repo. 16.9 MB allocated and freed in
  repo with 10 MB of incompressible data[cite: 3].

Demonstrates surgical memory management: zero leaks, zero use-after-free,
zero buffer overflows, even under high entropy torture and AES encryption[cite: 3].

valgrind --leak-check=full ./build_vg/baresnap init /tmp/vg_test[cite: 3]
Actual result (v2.3.5, build_vg)[cite: 3]:

==525052== HEAP SUMMARY:
==525052==     in use at exit: 0 bytes in 0 blocks
==525052==   total heap usage: 1 allocs, 1 frees, 64 bytes allocated
==525052==
==525052== All heap blocks were freed -- no leaks are possible
==525052==
==525052== ERROR SUMMARY: 0 errors from 0 contexts (suppressed: 0 from 0)[cite: 3]

============================================================================
11.13 PERFORMANCE
============================================================================

Tests performed on a CPU: Intel Core 2 Duo T7500 (2) @ 2.201GHz under
OS: Linux Mint 21 x86_64[cite: 3]
DE: MATE 1.26.0[cite: 3]
Kernel: 5.15.0-105-generic[cite: 3]
With BRAVE browser active, 4 tabs, and background browser processes[cite: 3].
Case 1: Info query with warm cache[cite: 3]
Command: baresnap info /repo[cite: 3]

- 106,676 entries[cite: 3]
- 34 packs[cite: 3]
- 2,768 cache entries[cite: 3]

Result with strace -c[cite: 3]:

- Total time: 38 milliseconds (0.038 seconds)[cite: 3].
- Only 108 read calls (efficient block reading)[cite: 3].
- 112 open/close (controlled descriptors, no leaks)[cite: 3].
- 1,368 mmap/munmap (efficient mapping of packs and index)[cite: 3].

Demonstrates that the C11 engine processes ~100k entries in memory in less than
40 ms once data is in the kernel's page cache[cite: 3].

Case 2: Real incremental backup with warm cache (LOCAL)[cite: 3]
Command: baresnap create /repo ~/proyectos[cite: 3]

- 2,872 entries processed[cite: 3]
- Cache: 2,629 hits (98.2%), 49 misses (1.8%)[cite: 3]
- Incremental pack generated: 157 KB (161,515 bytes)[cite: 3]

Result with time[cite: 3]:

- Real time:   0.872 seconds[cite: 3]
- User time:   0.438 seconds (50.2% of real time)[cite: 3]
- Sys time:    0.156 seconds (17.9% of real time)[cite: 3]
- Idle time:   0.278 seconds (31.9% waiting for I/O or sleep)[cite: 3]

Demonstrates that the C11 engine processes ~3,000 entries with a cache rate
of 98.2% in under 1 second, generating an incremental pack
of only 157 KB[cite: 3]. The low sys/real ratio (17.9%) indicates that I/O is
highly optimized with no unnecessary system calls[cite: 3].

Case 3: Incremental SSH backup (new in v2.1.4)[cite: 3]
Command: baresnap create ssh://servidor:/repo ~/proyectos[cite: 3]

- 2,953 entries processed[cite: 3]
- Cache: 2,713 hits (91.9%), 240 misses (8.1%)[cite: 3]
- Incremental pack generated: 1.0 KB (1,028 bytes)[cite: 3]
- Index segments: 9 (9 local cache, 0 downloaded)[cite: 3]

Result with time[cite: 3]:

- Real time:   0.354 seconds[cite: 3]
- User time:   0.180 seconds (50.8% of real time)[cite: 3]
- Sys time:    0.174 seconds (49.2% of real time)[cite: 3]
- SSH RPCs:      30 RPCs[cite: 3]
- Average latency: 82.9ms per RPC[cite: 3]
Comparison with previous version (v2.1.3)[cite: 3]:

- Before: 4.3 seconds, 60 RPCs, ~160ms average latency[cite: 3]
- Now: 0.35 seconds, 30 RPCs, ~83ms average latency[cite: 3]
- Improvement: 12.2x faster, 50% fewer RPCs[cite: 3]

Demonstrates that v2.1.4 SSH optimizations (local index cache,
delta disabling, 4MB write-back, local temp pack, elimination
of redundant fsyc) drastically reduce network latency[cite: 3].

============================================================================
12. SECURITY
============================================================================

NOTE v2.3.8[cite: 3]:
    Cryptographic foundations have not changed[cite: 3].

    Dual encryption is maintained[cite: 3]:

      - XChaCha20-Poly1305[cite: 3]
      - AES-256-GCM[cite: 3]

    An additional structural verification is added to the SSH flow[cite: 3]:
    before reusing cache or creating an incremental snapshot,
    BareSnap checks that all packs referenced by the
    existing index still exist[cite: 3].

    This does not alter cryptographic security, but avoids a class
    of logical corruption that only appeared remotely[cite: 3].

Encryption (Dual-Crypto since v2.2.0)[cite: 3]:
BareSnap supports two encryption algorithms selectable in init[cite: 3]:

1. XChaCha20-Poly1305 (default)[cite: 3]:

   - Algorithm: XChaCha20-Poly1305 (monocypher)[cite: 3]
   - Symmetric key: 32 bytes[cite: 3]
   - Nonce: 24 random bytes per chunk[cite: 3]
   - MAC: 16 bytes (Poly1305)[cite: 3]

2. AES-256-GCM (--encrypt aes)[cite: 3]:

   - Algorithm: AES-256 in GCM mode (Galois Counter Mode)[cite: 3]
   - Symmetric key: 32 bytes (256 bits)[cite: 3]
   - IV/Nonce: 12 random bytes per chunk[cite: 3]
   - Tag: 16 bytes (128 bits) GCM authentication[cite: 3]
   - Hardware acceleration: AES-NI + PCLMULQDQ on modern CPUs
     (automatic software fallback on older CPUs)[cite: 3]

Selection[cite: 3]:
    baresnap init /repo --encrypt            # XChaCha20-Poly1305 (default)[cite: 3]
    baresnap init /repo --encrypt aes        # AES-256-GCM[cite: 3]
    baresnap init /repo --encrypt chacha20   # XChaCha20-Poly1305 (explicit)[cite: 3]

The algorithm is stored in the repo config and used for ALL
chunks. Algorithms cannot be mixed in the same repo[cite: 3].

KVV (Key Verification Value)[cite: 3]:
Allows verifying the passphrase without storing it. A known block
is encrypted with the derived key. Upon opening the repo, it is
decrypted and checked. If it fails, the passphrase is incorrect[cite: 3].
Fixed 72-byte layout: nonce(24) + ciphertext(32) + mac(16)[cite: 3].

Secure wipe[cite: 3]:
brs_secure_wipe uses musl's native explicit_bzero[cite: 3].

Passphrase[cite: 3]:
Strict limit BRS_PASSPHRASE_MAX = 1024[cite: 3].
The passphrase is cleared with secure_zero() using a volatile pointer
to prevent GCC/Clang from eliminating the wipe under optimization[cite: 3].
Since v2.3.7 it can be injected via --pass-fd <N>, avoiding
appearance in argv or /proc/[PID]/cmdline[cite: 3].

Passphrase via file descriptor (--pass-fd)[cite: 3]:
Since v2.3.7, the passphrase can be injected from an anonymous file descriptor
in RAM, completely hiding it from /proc/[PID]/cmdline and
the visible command line in ps aux[cite: 3].

The fd is closed immediately after reading. The buffer is cleared
with secure_zero() in the cleanup registered with atexit(), guaranteeing
that the key does not survive in memory after process termination[cite: 3].

Recommended usage for production scripts[cite: 3]:
    PASS="$(systemd-creds cat baresnap-repo-key)"[cite: 3]
    baresnap --pass-fd 3 create /repo ~/docs 3<<<"$PASS"[cite: 3]
    unset PASS[cite: 3]

Exposure comparison[cite: 3]:
Method                    /proc/cmdline    /proc/environ      ps aux[cite: 3]
BARESNAP_PASSPHRASE=...   NO               YES                NO[cite: 3]
CLI Argument              YES              NO                 YES[cite: 3]
--pass-fd <N>             NO               NOT guaranteed     NO[cite: 3]

NOTE: --pass-fd prevents exposure in argv and ps aux. The fd is closed after
reading and the buffer is cleared with secure_zero()[cite: 3]. Absolute invisibility
in /proc/[PID]/environ is not promised if the engine internally reuses environment channels for compatibility[cite: 3].

============================================================================
12.1 RAM DEFENSES: ENGINE MEMORY FIREWALLS (V2.3.7)
============================================================================
BareSnap v2.3.7 documents three coordinated defenses to reduce
exposure of passphrases and sensitive data in RAM during the process lifecycle[cite: 3].

NOTE v2.3.8[cite: 3]:
    This section remains valid[cite: 3]. v2.3.8 changes do not modify
    existing RAM defenses[cite: 3].

------------------------------------------------------------
DEFENSE 1: IGNORED SIGPIPE
------------------------------------------------------------
Mechanism[cite: 3]:
signal(SIGPIPE, SIG_IGN);[cite: 3]

Problem[cite: 3]:
If an SSH pipe breaks, the default behavior of SIGPIPE is to
kill the process immediately. That can prevent the engine from going through
its normal cleanup routines[cite: 3].

Risks avoided[cite: 3]:
- unreleased lock file[cite: 3]
- orphaned temporaries[cite: 3]
- unclosed SSH VFS[cite: 3]
- passphrase uncleared via atexit()[cite: 3]

Effect[cite: 3]:
Writes/reads over the broken pipe return EPIPE in a
controlled manner[cite: 3]. The engine can execute cleanup, release locks, and clear RAM[cite: 3].

------------------------------------------------------------
DEFENSE 2: FD_CLOEXEC IN --pass-fd
------------------------------------------------------------
Mechanism[cite: 3]:
int flags = fcntl(fd, F_GETFD);[cite: 3]
if (flags != -1)[cite: 3]
    fcntl(fd, F_SETFD, flags | FD_CLOEXEC);[cite: 3]

Problem[cite: 3]:
In fork()+exec(), a child process can inherit open descriptors[cite: 3].
If the fd used by --pass-fd remained open, a child like ssh could
accidentally inherit it[cite: 3].

Effect[cite: 3]:
FD_CLOEXEC guarantees that the descriptor closes automatically upon exec()[cite: 3]. Additionally, BareSnap closes the fd immediately after reading the
passphrase[cite: 3].

Flow[cite: 3]:
1. shell opens fd N with passphrase[cite: 3]
2. baresnap receives --pass-fd N[cite: 3]
3. marks fd N with FD_CLOEXEC[cite: 3]
4. reads passphrase[cite: 3]
5. closes fd N[cite: 3]
6. clears buffer in cleanup_passphrase()[cite: 3]

------------------------------------------------------------
DEFENSE 3: secure_zero() WITH volatile POINTER
------------------------------------------------------------
Mechanism[cite: 3]:
static void secure_zero(void *p, size_t n)
{
    volatile unsigned char *s = (volatile unsigned char *)p;
    while (n--) {
        *s++ = 0;
    }
}[cite: 3]

Problem[cite: 3]:
A normal memset() before free() can be eliminated by the compiler
if it decides the write has no observable effect[cite: 3].

Effect[cite: 3]:
The volatile pointer forces real byte-by-byte writes[cite: 3]. The passphrase
is overwritten before freeing the buffer[cite: 3].

Usage points[cite: 3]:
- passphrase read by --pass-fd[cite: 3]
- askpass temporary buffers[cite: 3]
- derived keys / cryptographic buffers where appropriate[cite: 3]

------------------------------------------------------------
SUMMARY
------------------------------------------------------------
+----------------------+---------------------------+----------------------------+
| Defense              | Protects against          | Mechanism                  |
+----------------------+---------------------------+----------------------------+
| SIGPIPE SIG_IGN      | abrupt death by SSH       | signal(SIGPIPE, SIG_IGN)   |
| FD_CLOEXEC           | fd inheritance by children| fcntl(... FD_CLOEXEC)      |
| secure_zero volatile | optimized wipe outside    | volatile unsigned char *   |
+----------------------+---------------------------+----------------------------+[cite: 3]

The three defenses are complementary[cite: 3]:
- SIGPIPE forces controlled drop[cite: 3].
- FD_CLOEXEC avoids descriptor leakage to children[cite: 3].
- secure_zero avoids residual passphrase on heap[cite: 3].

SSH Password[cite: 3]:
The SSH password is read from /dev/tty with termios (no echo)[cite: 3].
It is never stored on disk or passed via command arguments[cite: 3].

For automation, BARESNAP_SSH_PASSWORD is read and cleared
from the environment after use[cite: 3].

Paths[cite: 3]:
brs_is_safe_relative_path rejects absolute paths and traversal[cite: 3].

Anti-OOM and anti-DoS[cite: 3]:
BRS_CHUNK_UNCOMP_MAX (64 MB) validates the uncompressed size
BEFORE allocating memory[cite: 3].

Remote agent[cite: 3]:
The `baresnap-remote` agent runs on the server with the
permissions of the user initiating the SSH connection[cite: 3]. It rejects
any path containing ".." or absolute paths, guaranteeing
that it can only operate within the repository directory[cite: 3].

    chunk_min = 16384   (16 KB)[cite: 3]

    chunk_max = 262144  (256 KB)[cite: 3]

    PACK_READ_MAX_BYTES        256 MB[cite: 3]

BRS_RAM_GUARD_THRESHOLD_BYTES  600 MB[cite: 3]
BRS_ZSTD_ULTRA_MIN_LEVEL       20[cite: 3]
BRS_ZSTD_SAFE_LEVEL            19[cite: 3]

    - BRS_PACK_MAX_SIZE is no longer 64 MB; rotation occurs at
      100 MB (BRS_PACK_ROTATE_BYTES)[cite: 3].
    - The Bloom filter now uses 16 Mbits per segment and 5 hashes[cite: 3].
    - BRS_FOOTER_ENTRY_SIZE = 33 (corrected)[cite: 3].

============================================================================
13.1 SESSION ENVIRONMENT VARIABLES (NEW IN V2.3.0)
============================================================================

    BRS_CREATE_COMPRESSION      Forces session algorithm (none | lz4 | zstd)[cite: 3]
    BRS_CREATE_ZSTD_LEVEL       Forces session ZSTD level (Strict range 1-22)[cite: 3]

============================================================================
13.1b SAFEGUARD ENVIRONMENT VARIABLES (NEW IN V2.3.5)
============================================================================
BRS_RAM_GUARD_FAKE_MB
Simulates available memory (in MB) to force or inhibit the RAM guardian
of FIX O1 in tests[cite: 3]. Numeric value in MB[cite: 3]. If defined and >= 0,
brs_available_ram_bytes() returns that value instead of reading /proc/meminfo
or sysconf[cite: 3]. Exclusive use of mega_test section 108[cite: 3].

Example: BRS_RAM_GUARD_FAKE_MB=100 ./baresnap create ... --zstd-level 22[cite: 3]
============================================================================

============================================================================
13.2 SSH ENVIRONMENT VARIABLES (V2.1.4 / V2.2.2)
============================================================================

BRS_SSH_STRICT_FSYNC        Disables lazy fsync mode over SSH if set to 1[cite: 3]
BRS_SSH_DEBUG               Displays raw RPC trace on stderr if set to 1[cite: 3]

NOTE (v2.3.6): BRS_SSH_TIMEOUT_MS has been removed[cite: 3]. The SSH timeout
is now configured via the global CLI flag --timeout <ms>[cite: 3].
See section 8 for usage examples[cite: 3].

==============================================================================
14. ENVIRONMENT CONTROL VARIABLES (NEW IN V2.3.0 / V2.2.2)
==============================================================================

BRS_CREATE_COMPRESSION      Forces session algorithm (none | lz4 | zstd) (v2.3.0)[cite: 3]
BRS_CREATE_ZSTD_LEVEL       Forces session ZSTD level (Strict range 1-22) (v2.3.0)[cite: 3]
BRS_SSH_STRICT_FSYNC        Disables lazy fsync mode over SSH if set to 1 (v2.1.4)[cite: 3]
BRS_SSH_DEBUG               Displays raw RPC trace on stderr if set to 1 (v2.1.4)[cite: 3]
BRS_RAM_GUARD_FAKE_MB       Simulates available RAM for the O1 guardian (v2.3.5)[cite: 3]

NOTE (v2.3.6): BRS_SSH_TIMEOUT_MS removed in favor of CLI flag --timeout[cite: 3].
==============================================================================
15. TROUBLESHOOTING
==============================================================================

GUI (yad) does not open[cite: 3]:
Verify yad is installed: which yad[cite: 3]
On Debian/Ubuntu: apt install yad[cite: 3]

TUI does not open / crashes on startup[cite: 3]:
Verify the terminal supports ncurses and colors[cite: 3].
Try with TERM=xterm-256color[cite: 3].

F10 does not work in TUI[cite: 3]:
Some terminals (MATE, GNOME) capture F10 for their own window menu[cite: 3].
Use Ctrl+Q as an alternative to exit the TUI[cite: 3].

TUI picker does not show any [REPO][cite: 3]:
The picker only marks directories containing a valid "config"
file as [REPO][cite: 3].

ZSTD compresses worse than LZ4 on random data[cite: 3]:
Expected behavior[cite: 3]. ZSTD shines with compressible data
(text, code, logs)[cite: 3]. On random data (photos, video, .bin
urandom) both codecs store the chunk uncompressed[cite: 3].

Bloom filter gives false positives[cite: 3]:
By design (<0.01%)[cite: 3]. A false positive only causes extra
disk load (.idx is loaded to verify), never a deduplication error
or data loss[cite: 3].

create with --compression on an existing repo[cite: 3]:
A warning is emitted and the CLI option is ignored[cite: 3]. Base compression is a
repo property set at init[cite: 3]. However, since v2.3.0 you can force
a different algorithm for the current session using environment variables
BRS_CREATE_COMPRESSION and BRS_CREATE_ZSTD_LEVEL[cite: 3].

Failure in remote commands (SSH) with password[cite: 3]:
If password authentication fails, verify[cite: 3]:
1. That the SSH server accepts passwords[cite: 3]:
   PasswordAuthentication yes in /etc/ssh/sshd_config[cite: 3]
2. That /dev/tty is accessible to the user[cite: 3].
3. That the askpass wrapper was generated correctly[cite: 3]:
   ls -la ~/.local/libexec/baresnap-askpass[cite: 3]
4. For automation, use BARESNAP_SSH_PASSWORD[cite: 3].

The `remote install` command must be executed before any
`init` or `create` on a new remote repository[cite: 3].

Current SSH behavior[cite: 3]:

- 2-second poll() on initial handshake[cite: 3].
- If the agent dies, POLLHUP breaks the operation and the client aborts cleanly[cite: 3].
- Agent execution is anchored to the repo[cite: 3]:
  cd '<repo_path>' && exec ./.local/bin/baresnap-remote --repo .[cite: 3]

- In backups with 100% deduplication, the packer isolates itself and
  does not attempt to upload empty packs[cite: 3].

- To diagnose latency: BRS_SSH_DEBUG=1[cite: 3].
Health with incorrect passphrase generates false positive (RESOLVED v2.3.1)[cite: 3]:
Prior to v2.3.1, running 'baresnap health' with an incorrect passphrase
on an encrypted repo could generate a false positive corruption alert
and move healthy snapshots to damaged/ during --repair[cite: 3]. Since v2.3.1, the
engine detects that the key is invalid (derive_repo_key fails) and aborts
with a clean error (rc=1) without touching any snapshot[cite: 3]. The repository
remains intact[cite: 3].

Index map grow with millions of chunks (RESOLVED v2.3.1)[cite: 3]:
The index_map_grow() function called brs_index_map_put() to re-insert
entries during re-hash[cite: 3]. This caused a recursive call to
index_map_grow if the new array still exceeded 70% load, potentially
causing OOM in repos with millions of chunks[cite: 3]. Since v2.3.0, re-insertion
is inline: hash is calculated and linear probing is done directly on
the new array, without going through brs_index_map_put()[cite: 3].

============================================================================
15.1 FALSE POSITIVE CONTROL IN BLOOM FILTER
============================================================================

Since the .blm file is a probabilistic structure, a nominal
false positive rate (< 0.1%) exists[cite: 3]. If the filter falsely indicates that a
chunk already exists in the remote repository, BareSnap does not abort the operation[cite: 3].

Strict Double Verification: Upon a positive match in the Bloom
filter, the engine executes a mandatory secondary fallback: it performs an
indexed query against the real strong hash .idx file
(BLAKE2B-128)[cite: 3].

Impact: The risk of silent corruption (omission of new chunks
in the snapshot) is 100% eliminated[cite: 3]. The Bloom filter maintains its function of
quick discard accelerator without compromising physical data consistency[cite: 3].

==============================================================================
16. DESIGN PHILOSOPHY
==============================================================================

BareSnap was born as a fork of an existing backup and was
completely rewritten in C11 with a clear goal: zero
dependencies, static binary, documented binary format[cite: 3].

"When the cloud goes down and package managers fail,
the Spartan tool remains."[cite: 3]

Base compression is a repository property, fixed at init[cite: 3].

This simplifies default logic[cite: 3]. However, since v2.3.0 the
pipeline allows forcing algorithms per session, enabling LZ4
and ZSTD chunks to coexist in the same repository[cite: 3]. The per-chunk flag
(BRS_CHUNK_FLAG_ZSTD) enables automatic detection in
restore/verify without needing to read config first[cite: 3].

The Bloom Filter follows the same philosophy: minimum impact on
complexity, maximum impact on scalability[cite: 3]. 128 KB per segment
is a fixed and predictable cost[cite: 3]. Lazy loading is only activated
in create; restore/verify remain simple and direct[cite: 3].

SSH support was implemented via a lightweight agent (baresnap-remote)
that communicates via a binary protocol over stdin/stdout, avoiding
SFTP overhead and maintaining POSIX semantics (hardlinks,
atomic rename) whenever the remote filesystem permits it[cite: 3].

SSH password authentication was implemented without external dependencies
(no sshpass, no expect): the binary itself acts as an
askpass helper, reading the password from /dev/tty with termios[cite: 3].

This maintains the "zero dependencies" philosophy even for
authentication[cite: 3].

v2.1.4 SSH optimizations (local cache, batching, temp pack)
follow the same philosophy: maximum performance without external dependencies
or complex protocols[cite: 3].

============================================================================
16.1 CONSCIOUS ARCHITECTURAL LIMITATIONS
============================================================================


- Cryptographic Agility (RESOLVED in v2.2.0)[cite: 3]:
  Since v2.2.0, BareSnap supports dual-crypto: XChaCha20-Poly1305 (default)
  and AES-256-GCM (selectable with --encrypt aes)[cite: 3]. The algorithm is fixed
  at init and stored in the repo config[cite: 3]. AES-NI/PCLMULQDQ detection
  is automatic via CPUID, with transparent software fallback
  on older CPUs (Core 2 Duo, etc.)[cite: 3].
  Tests 71-79 certify: zero plaintext leaks, blocking of
  false passphrases, bit rot detection via GCM Tag, 1-byte mutation
  resistance, and power-cut survival[cite: 3].

- Platform Exclusion: BareSnap is Linux-only by design decision[cite: 3].
  The strict use of specific POSIX calls (getrandom, utimensat, /dev/tty)
  rules out macOS and Windows[cite: 3]. This limits its viability as a universal
  corporate tool for mixed parks, but guarantees performance and
  Spartan simplicity in pure Linux environments[cite: 3]. For heterogeneous
  infrastructures, using BareSnap on Linux nodes and another
  tool for Windows/macOS is recommended[cite: 3].

==============================================================================
17. EDGE CASES AND MEMORY LIMITS
==============================================================================

Index segment and memory limits[cite: 3]:
The index is divided into segments (.idx) to avoid loading the entire
map into memory all at once during writes[cite: 3].

With Bloom Filters[cite: 3]:

- create loads only .blm files (~128 KB per segment)[cite: 3]
- .idx files are loaded on demand when a bloom indicates a hit[cite: 3]
- For 10M chunks: ~12 MB of blooms vs ~500 MB of complete index[cite: 3]

Without Bloom Filters (restore/verify/extract)[cite: 3]:

- Loads the complete index (brs_load_all_indexes)[cite: 3]
- Necessary for exact offset resolution[cite: 3]

Cancellation mid-pack[cite: 3]:
If Ctrl+C arrives during create, the temporary pack remains in tmp/
as pack_<id>.tmp[cite: 3]. The next create cleans tmp/*.tmp upon
startup (anti-power cut)[cite: 3].

Inconsistent file cache[cite: 3]:
If cache references chunks that no longer exist in the index,
create detects it entry by entry (all_present with
lazy_get_main) and treats the file as a cache miss[cite: 3].

Orphaned Bloom filter after prune[cite: 3]:
Prune deletes .blm files along with old .idx files[cite: 3]. Additionally, create
filters out blooms whose .idx no longer exists on disk[cite: 3].

Power cut during concurrent Prune[cite: 3]:
Concurrent prune uses 2 workers that rewrite packs in parallel[cite: 3].

The safety pattern is "write new BEFORE deleting old"[cite: 3]:
1. New packs: written as pack_<id>.pack directly (the
   writer uses write_file_atomic which does write-to-tmp + rename)[cite: 3].
   If cut arrives during write, .tmp remains orphaned
   and is cleaned in the next create[cite: 3]. The old pack is NEVER deleted
   until the new one is complete and the index updated[cite: 3].
2. New index: brs_write_index_segment writes the new .idx with
   write_file_atomic[cite: 3]. The old .idx deletion loop runs
   AFTER. If cut arrives between writing the new and
   deleting the old, the next create will see duplicate but
   harmless .idx files (entries are overwritten in
   the hash map by key)[cite: 3].
3. Bloom Filters: new .blm files are generated in
   brs_write_index_segment[cite: 3]. If cut arrives before they are
   written, the next create will not find a .blm for that
   segment and will fallback to brs_load_all_indexes (full load)[cite: 3].
   Orphaned .blm files (whose .idx no longer exists) are filtered out in
   create's bloom load loop[cite: 3].
4. File cache: may become inconsistent if cut arrives during
   dead entry cleanup[cite: 3]. The next create detects cache entries
   whose chunks do not exist in the index
   (all_present with lazy_get_main) and treats them as cache miss[cite: 3].
   Never causes incorrect data, only extra re-chunking[cite: 3].
5. Snapshots: snapshot deletion is done BEFORE
   pack rewriting[cite: 3]. If cut arrives after deleting
   snapshots but before rewriting packs, those snapshots' chunks
   remain as "dead" in packs[cite: 3]. The next prune
   will detect and rewrite them[cite: 3]. No data loss occurs because
   KEPT snapshots were processed first[cite: 3].

Global guarantee: a power cut NEVER leaves a repo in an
irreparable state[cite: 3]. In the worst case, there are packs with extra dead bytes
that the next prune will clean[cite: 3]. The integrity of live
data is guaranteed by the write-before-delete pattern[cite: 3].

Bloom Filter False Positive in Cache Verification[cite: 3]:
A false positive occurs when brs_bloom_check() returns 1
("possibly exists") for a chunk that actually does NOT exist in
that index segment[cite: 3].

Engine behavior (Strict Double Verification)[cite: 3]:
1. In create, cache verification uses lazy_get_main()[cite: 3]:

   - Queries existing_index (already loaded segments)[cite: 3].
   - If not present, queries each bloom with brs_bloom_check()[cite: 3].
   - If a bloom says "possible hit", that .idx is loaded with
     brs_load_index_segment() under mutex[cite: 3].

   - Queries existing_index again[cite: 3]. If the chunk is NOT
     present (false positive), it is simply not found and treated as new[cite: 3].

2. Consequence of false positive[cite: 3]:

   - An .idx was loaded unnecessarily (~50-200 KB extra I/O)[cite: 3].
   - The chunk is treated as "new" and re-chunked[cite: 3].
   - NO data loss or incorrect deduplication occurs[cite: 3]. Double
     verification against the real .idx 100% eliminates the risk of
     silent corruption[cite: 3].

3. What can NEVER happen[cite: 3]:

   - A false NEGATIVE (bloom says "does not exist" but it does)[cite: 3].
     Matematically impossible: if a chunk was inserted, its
     7 bits are 1[cite: 3]. brs_bloom_check only returns 0 if some
     bit is 0, which only happens if the chunk was NOT inserted[cite: 3].

   - Data corruption: the bloom only decides WHETHER to load an .idx,
     never what data to return[cite: 3]. Final resolution is always done by
     brs_index_map_get() on the loaded .idx[cite: 3].

4. Quantitative impact[cite: 3]:

   - With 1M bits and 7 hashes: P(fp) ≈ 0.009% per query[cite: 3].
   - For a create with 100,000 new chunks: ~9 false positives[cite: 3].
   - Each false positive causes ~1 .idx load (~100 KB I/O)[cite: 3].
   - Total overhead: ~900 KB extra I/O in a 100K chunk create[cite: 3]. Negligible compared to backup GBs[cite: 3].

5. In restore/verify/extract[cite: 3]:

   - These operations DO NOT use Bloom Filters[cite: 3]. They load the complete
     index with brs_load_all_indexes() because they need
     exact offset resolution for all chunks[cite: 3].

   - The bloom is only used in create (where most chunks
     are new and do not need exact resolution)[cite: 3].


============================================================================
17.1 "REPO FRANKENSTEIN" STRUCTURAL ANALYSIS
============================================================================

A "Frankenstein Repo" is an extreme survival state where the
repository has lost its control metadata (config and index) but
maintains physical payloads (packs) and manifests (snapshots) intact[cite: 3].

Trigger scenario[cite: 3]:

- Manual deletion of config and index/ directory[cite: 3].
- Survival of packs/ and snapshots/[cite: 3].
- (Contrast) If index/ exists but NO config -> "Inconsistent Repo"[cite: 3].

Data Structural Analysis[cite: 3]:
1. PACKS (Survivors)[cite: 3]:
   Packs are append-only containers[cite: 3]. Each pack contains in its
   footer an internal index (offsets, sizes, flags) and a CRC32C[cite: 3].
   Mathematically, payloads are 100% intact and independently
   verifiable (CRC32C + GCM/ChaCha20 Tags per chunk)[cite: 3].

2. SNAPSHOTS (Survivors)[cite: 3]:
   Snapshots contain chunk_ids (BLAKE2B-128) and metadata[cite: 3].
   We know WHAT data existed at backup time, but not
   WHERE it is physically stored[cite: 3].

3. CONFIG (Lost)[cite: 3]:
   Without config, repo UUID, Argon2 salt, and KVV are lost[cite: 3].

   - If repo was NOT encrypted: Payloads are directly readable[cite: 3].
   - If repo WAS encrypted: Without original salt, key derivation
     will generate a different key[cite: 3]. Payloads are
     cryptographically inaccessible (unrecoverable)[cite: 3].

Engine Resolution (Controlled Auto-init)[cite: 3]:

- If NO index/ or metadata: The engine assumes it can auto-initialize[cite: 3].
  Scans all packs/ reading their footers to rebuild
  .idx and .blm segments from scratch[cite: 3]. Repo comes back to life[cite: 3].

- If INDEX/ exists but NO config: The engine ABORTS ("inconsistent state")[cite: 3].
  This protects an existing repo that might contain unrecoverable encrypted data[cite: 3]. health --repair is mandatory to recreate config[cite: 3].

- If index and config exist, but a referenced pack is missing
  (v2.3.8)[cite: 3]: create aborts with a clear error[cite: 3]:

      error: pack file 'packs/...' does not exist[cite: 3]

  health detects the anomaly and health --repair moves affected
  snapshots to damaged/[cite: 3].

  Prior to v2.3.8, this case could be masked by cache and
  generate fake snapshots pointing to non-existent packs[cite: 3].

Guarantee: In unencrypted repos, a Frankenstein Repo is 100% automatically
recoverable[cite: 3]. In encrypted repos, structure is recovered but
data requires original passphrase and salt (if config is lost,
encrypted data is unrecoverable by design)[cite: 3].

###############################################################################
CRITICAL RECOVERY WARNING: THE ENCRYPTED FRANKENSTEIN REPO
###############################################################################
The BareSnap engine is capable of reconstructing a repository that has lost its
'config' file and 'index/' directory via raw scanning of '.pack' file
footers (Controlled auto-init via health --repair)[cite: 3].
THIS RULE ONLY GUARANTEES FULL RECOVERY IN FLAT REPOSITORIES[cite: 3].

If the original repository was initialized with encryption (--encrypt)[cite: 3]:
1. The 'config' file contains the Argon2id Salt and essential KVV[cite: 3].
2. If you destroy or manually delete the central 'config' file, future
   cryptographic derivation will generate a different mathematical key,
   even if you enter the correct passphrase[cite: 3].
3. Pack physical payloads will become UNRECOVERABLE by design[cite: 3].

FIX F1 (v2.3.5): health --repair no longer blindly recreates a plain config[cite: 3].
Before recreating it, health_detect_encrypted_snapshots() scans snapshots
looking for encrypted magic (BRSNAP2\x01)[cite: 3]. If found, the recreated config
is marked with encrypted=1 and an explicit warning is emitted[cite: 3], avoiding
leaving the repo silenced as "plain" when its actual content is encrypted,
eliminating the silent corruption path[cite: 3]. Structure and flag are
recovered; payloads still depend on the original passphrase[cite: 3].

PRODUCTION MAXIMUM: Protect the 'config' file as much as your data[cite: 3].
If you run failure tests with included scripts, do them in /tmp environments[cite: 3].
###############################################################################
